But then I wake up feeling so bloated and sluggish, like I mentioned before, and I just know it's taking a toll on my body internally, even if the scale isn't moving. So, I guess the short answer is no, no major changes in the actual number, which is honestly a miracle considering the sheer amount of food I'm putting away.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating happen?\n\nPatient: How often? Gosh, um... if I really sit back and think about it, looking at the calendar... it's been a solid three months of this. Like, really consistently for the last three months. I mean, I think it really ramped up around the time we got the new quarterly projections at the agency and my husband started getting more vocal about my hours. \n\nSo, yeah, for the last three months, I'd say there is at least one night, almost every single week, where I end up in the kitchen doing exactly what I described. Just eating like that until I can't anymore. And honestly, some weeks it's worse. Some weeks it happens twice. \n\nIt almost always follows a specific pattern, too. It's never just a random, peaceful Tuesday where everything was fine. It's pretty much always right after a tense argument with my husband—you know, one of those fights where he gives me that heavy sigh and just walks away upstairs—or it's after a really late work event. Like, if I've been at the agency smiling and pitching that AR/VR campaign until 8 or 9 PM, and I just come home completely, utterly depleted. I'll walk in the door, the house is quiet, he's already asleep or ignoring me, and I just... I gravitate right to the pantry. \n\nAnd I know once a week, or twice a week, might not sound like an everyday, constant thing on paper, but when you factor in the emotional toll? The hangover feeling the next morning, the sneaking around, the shame I was telling you about... it feels like it consumes my entire week. Because if it happens on a Thursday night after a late client dinner, then Friday is ruined because I'm exhausted and bloated at my desk, and then by Saturday I'm canceling my yoga class because I still feel disgusting. So even if the actual eating episode is only once or twice a week, the ripple effect of it is just... it's always there. It feels like I never really get a break from the cycle of it, you know? It's just always hanging over me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often criticize yourself after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Criticize myself? Oh, God, yes. Constantly. It's almost worse than the eating itself, to be completely honest. Afterward, when the food is gone and I'm just standing there looking at the empty wrappers or the crumbs on the counter... the wave of embarrassment that washes over me is just suffocating. I feel so utterly disgusted with myself. I mean, I will literally look down at my hands and wonder what on earth is wrong with me. \n\nBecause, you have to understand, in every other aspect of my life, I am so put together. I manage a whole team at the agency, I handle massive budgets, I'm the one people come to when they need a crisis solved right that second. I really pride myself on being this highly disciplined, capable woman. And then I have these moments where I completely lose control over a bag of pretzels or a box of cookies? It feels like I've just blown that entire persona. Like the disciplined person I try so hard to be is just a total fraud, and it all falls apart the second I am alone in my kitchen.\n\nAnd then the guilt sets in, and I will just sit there at the kitchen island, staring into space, just relentlessly beating myself up. I will run through all the things I should have done instead of eating. I will sit there thinking I should have just gone for a run to blow off steam, or I should have unrolled my mat and done some yoga in the living room to decompress. Why couldn't I just do some deep breathing like a normal stressed person, you know? But instead, I did this. And that self-criticism just loops in my head for hours. It bleeds into the next day. I will be sitting in a morning meeting, reviewing ad copy, and in the back of my mind, I am just tearing myself apart, calling myself weak and disgusting over and over again. It is just a relentless inner monologue.\n\nDoctor: Do you criticize yourself immediately after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Immediately? Yes, exactly, like I was just saying, it’s... it is instant. There is absolutely zero delay. The very second I swallow the last bite, or the moment my hand hits the bottom of the empty bag... boom. It hits me like a freight train. \n\nThat embarrassment and disgust I was just telling you about? It doesn't wait until the next morning or even until I go upstairs to bed. It is right there, immediately. I'll literally still be standing there in the kitchen, the taste still in my mouth, and I just feel so profoundly embarrassed. I look at the mess on the counter and think about how I’ve completely blown it. Like I said, I try so incredibly hard all day long to be this highly disciplined, put-together person who has all the answers for everyone else, and in that immediate aftermath, I just feel like a total failure. The contrast between who I am at 2 PM and who I am at 10 PM is just jarring.\n\nAnd right then and there, without even leaving the kitchen, I'll start the spiral. I'll just sit there at the island, staring at the empty wrappers, immediately punishing myself. I'll sit there thinking, \"Why didn't I just go for a run? I could have just put on my sneakers and jogged around the neighborhood to clear my head.\" Or I'll look over at the living room and think, \"I have that expensive yoga mat rolled up right there in the corner, why didn't I just go do some yoga instead of doing this?\" It's instantaneous. There is no buffer at all between the end of the eating and the absolute disgust with myself. It's just an immediate crash.\n\nDoctor: Does the self-criticism you feel after these episodes of eating tend to last for a while after you've gone to bed, or even into the next day?\n\nPatient: Oh, it definitely lasts. It doesn't just stop when I finally drag myself upstairs to bed, not at all. I'll lie there in the dark, staring at the ceiling, my husband asleep next to me, and my brain is just... it's just spinning. Replaying the whole thing. And then, yes, it absolutely bleeds into the next day. It's like this heavy, dark cloud that I wake up under. \n\nI mean, I'm still keeping up with work, thank goodness. I still show up at the agency, I still run the meetings, I still smile and nod and approve the ad copy. I haven't let the balls drop there, because I can't. I'm the manager, I have to be on. But underneath that? The mornings after these nights, I just feel completely drained. It's this bone-deep exhaustion, mixed with just... so much shame. I'll be sitting at my desk, looking at spreadsheets, and I just feel gross in my own skin. \n\nAnd it's starting to affect the things I actually enjoy doing, too. Like, I was telling you about my yoga class? I used to love going to this Saturday morning vinyasa flow. It was my time to just breathe and reset. But lately, if I've had one of these episodes on a Thursday or Friday, I'll just cancel. I'll wake up Saturday morning, still feeling that hangover of shame and bloat, and I just... I can't do it. I don't want to put on the tight leggings, I don't want to be in a room full of people, I just don't want to face anyone. I've even started skipping my weekend creative time, where I usually sketch or brainstorm personal projects, because my mind is just so bogged down by the guilt. I just want to hide. So, yeah, it definitely doesn't just stay in the kitchen. It follows me around for days.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating in response to your emotions during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Emotions? I... I don't know. I mean, I guess it's possible, but I really haven't noticed a clear pattern like that. It's not like I have a bad meeting and immediately think, \"I need to go eat a box of cookies.\" \n\nI mean, sure, I'm stressed. Who isn't stressed in my line of work? I manage a huge team, the clients are always demanding revisions at the eleventh hour, the budgets are tight... it's a pressure cooker. And I'm the one everyone looks to for answers. So, yes, there's stress. But I don't feel like I'm eating *because* I'm stressed, if that makes sense? \n\nIt's more like... it just happens. I'll get home, I'll put my bag down, I'll start sorting through the mail or wiping down the counter, and then suddenly I'm just... eating. I'm not standing there crying over a breakup or fuming over a rude email from a client. I'm just... there. And then the eating starts. So, I don't know if I'd say it's an emotional response. I haven't really connected it to feeling sad or angry or anything specific like that. It feels more like this weird, automatic thing that just takes over, rather than me trying to soothe some specific emotion. I honestly haven't really thought about it in those terms before.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean, like I was just saying, I really haven't connected the dots between specific emotions and the eating. You're asking about anxiety, sadness, frustration... and it's just, it's not like that for me. I don't sit there and think, "Oh, I'm so frustrated that the Johnson account fell through, I'm going to eat a whole sleeve of crackers." It's just not this conscious, one-to-one reaction. 

I mean, if we're talking about anxiety, well, my baseline is pretty much always a low hum of anxiety, right? I'm a marketing manager, I'm 40 years old, I'm trying to keep a dozen plates spinning at once, both at the agency and at home. So, am I anxious? Sure. Am I frustrated when my junior copywriter misses a deadline for the third time this month? Absolutely. Am I sad? Sometimes, yeah, like anyone else. But I don't feel like those feelings are the *trigger*. 

It's more like... I'll just be going through the motions of my evening. Like, maybe it's a Tuesday, and I've just finished a two-hour Zoom call that could have been an email, and I'm tired, but I'm not actively, intensely feeling a specific emotion. I'm just existing. I'll walk into the kitchen to maybe get a glass of water, and then it's like a switch flips. Suddenly I'm opening the pantry, and then the fridge, and it's just... happening. I'm not doing it to push down a feeling of sadness or to calm my nerves. It's almost like I'm disconnected from my emotions entirely in that moment. It's just this incredibly powerful, automatic drive that takes over, completely separate from whatever I might have been feeling five minutes prior. So, no, I really don't think it's triggered by specific emotions like that. It just doesn't feel like an emotional response to me.

We have been bickering a lot lately, mostly because I am so stressed from the agency and he feels like I am not present, which just creates this awful, heavy atmosphere in the house. So after he finally goes upstairs to bed and that tension is just hanging in the air, I start eating and it feels like... I don't even know how to describe it accurately. It feels like the whole decision-making part of my brain just completely goes offline. \n\nI can literally be standing there at the kitchen island, and I am actively thinking to myself, Patricia, stop, this is enough, you do not need another bite of this. I am fully aware that I should stop, and I am telling myself to stop. But my hand just keeps reaching for more anyway. It is like a total disconnect between my mind and my body. I just keep opening wrappers or scooping out more food, completely ignoring my own internal voice begging me to quit. I just can't seem to halt the momentum once it gets going, no matter how hard I try to reason with myself in the moment. I just watch myself doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Oh, yes. The guilt is... it's absolutely overwhelming. And it's not just guilt, it's this profound sense of embarrassment and just utter disgust with myself. It hits me the second I stop eating, like a ton of bricks. I'll be standing there in the kitchen, surrounded by all the empty wrappers and Tupperware containers, and I just look at the mess and think, \"What have I done?\" \n\nI mean, I spend my entire day at the agency projecting this image of someone who is totally put together, disciplined, in charge. I'm the one managing the budgets, directing the campaigns, keeping everyone else on track. And then I come home and do this? It feels like I've completely blown that entire persona, like the disciplined person I try so hard to be all day is just a total fraud. \n\nI'll just sit there at the kitchen counter, feeling physically sick and emotionally drained, and my mind just starts racing with all these \"should haves.\" I'll sit there thinking, \"Patricia, you should have just gone for a run when you got home. You should have gone upstairs and done 20 minutes of yoga. You could have read a book, taken a bath, done literally anything else.\" But instead, I just stood there and ate thousands of calories in secret. And the worst part is knowing that I'm going to have to wake up the next morning, put on my suit, and pretend like none of it happened. It's just this cycle of disgust that I can't seem to break out of, and it hangs over me the entire next day.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: No, no, I've never done that. I mean, I know that's something people do, and honestly, sometimes when I'm sitting there feeling so incredibly full and just physically sick from all the food, the thought crosses my mind that maybe it would make the physical discomfort go away. Because the fullness is just so intense, you know? It physically hurts. My stomach gets so bloated and tight, and I just feel so heavy and sluggish, like I can barely move from the kitchen stool. \n\nBut no, I have never actually made myself throw up. I just... I couldn't bring myself to do it. Even though I feel so disgusted with myself, and I desperately want to undo what I just did, I just end up sitting there with it. I sit there with the physical pain and the emotional guilt. I'll usually just end up drinking a huge glass of water, which probably makes the bloating worse, honestly, and then I just drag myself upstairs to bed and lie there feeling awful until I finally fall asleep. I just kind of force myself to suffer through the physical consequences of it, I guess. It's almost like a punishment I feel like I deserve for losing control like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel extremely hungry before these episodes of eating happen?\n\nPatient: Hungry? Well... no, not really. I mean, it's not about hunger. It's almost never about actual, physical hunger. Sometimes I've even just had dinner with my husband—maybe we ordered takeout or I threw together a salad, you know, a completely normal, filling meal. So my stomach isn't growling or anything like that. \n\nBut it's this... this mental hunger, I guess? Or like an emotional craving that just suddenly takes over. I'll be cleaning up the kitchen, wiping down the counters, putting the plates in the dishwasher, and all of a sudden, it's just this overwhelming urge. It's not that my body needs fuel. It's more like my brain is screaming for a distraction or a way to numb out the stress from the day, the tension in the house, the emails I still have to answer for the morning meeting. \n\nSo, no, I wouldn't say I'm extremely hungry. Or even mildly hungry, most of the time. I could be completely full from dinner and still end up standing at the pantry ten minutes later, eating half a box of crackers before I even realize what I'm doing. It's just so frustrating because if it were just hunger, I could fix that, right? I could just eat a proper meal. But it's not. It's something else entirely, and it just feels so impossible to satisfy.\n\nDoctor: Does your eating behavior change when you're under a lot of stress at work?\n\nPatient: Stress? Oh, absolutely. Stress is... it's the whole trigger, really. I mean, my job is always demanding, but lately, it's just been on another level. This whole pattern of coming home and just losing myself in the pantry... it really started when this massive AR/VR campaign ramped up at the agency. We're talking about a multi-million dollar account, and the client is just incredibly demanding. They want constant updates, revisions, new pitch decks every other day. I'm managing a team of twelve, and the pressure is just astronomical. \n\nAnd then, on top of all that pressure at the office, things at home have been so incredibly tense. My husband and I, we've just been arguing constantly. It's about everything and nothing—the house, the finances, the fact that I'm working so much, the fact that he feels like I'm not present even when I am home. It's just this constant, low-level hostility that spikes into these really awful fights. \n\nSo you take the stress from the AR/VR launch, and you combine it with the arguments at home getting so much worse, and it was just... a perfect storm. That was about three months ago. That's when I really noticed this starting. Three months of just coming home, feeling like I've been put through a wringer all day, walking into a house where I feel like I'm walking on eggshells, and then just... heading straight for the kitchen the minute I have a moment alone. It's like the food is the only thing that gives me a break from the pressure, even if it's just for those ten or twenty minutes before the guilt sets in. But yeah, to answer your question, three months ago is when the stress really peaked and this whole nightmare started.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating very quickly during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Quickly? Oh, gosh, yes. It’s... it’s honestly like a blur. I don’t even take the time to sit down at the table or get a proper plate most of the time. I’ll just be standing there, right at the kitchen island, usually with my phone in one hand because I'm just obsessively scrolling through work emails or checking the latest Slack messages from the creative team. And while I’m doing that, reading about whatever new crisis is happening with the pitch deck, I’m just eating so incredibly fast. It's almost frantic. \n\nI shove it in so quickly that I barely even taste it. You'd think if you were going to eat all that food, you'd at least get the pleasure of enjoying the flavor, right? But I don't. I'm not savoring it at all. It's just this mechanical, rapid motion, almost like I'm on autopilot. And it’s like I completely zone out, because sometimes I’ll finally look down, pulling my eyes away from the screen for a second, and the containers are just... empty. The whole box or the entire bag, just completely gone. And I just stand there staring at the empty packaging because I haven’t even really registered what I was eating. It happens so fast that my brain doesn't even have time to catch up with what my hands and my mouth were doing. I'll just be standing there in the kitchen, holding an empty container, wondering how on earth I just ate all of that in a matter of minutes.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you used to enjoy because of your eating habits?\n\nPatient: Lost interest? I don't know if \"lost interest\" is the right phrase, but... it's definitely affecting what I do. I mean, work is still happening. I'm still showing up for the agency, still leading the team meetings, still pushing through the AR/VR campaign because I have to. There's too much money on the line for me to just drop the ball there. I can put on the blazer and the professional face and get through the day. \n\nBut the mornings after these nights... after I've just stood in the kitchen and eaten everything... I wake up feeling so incredibly drained. It's this heavy, sluggish feeling, like a hangover, but worse because it's entirely my own fault. And the shame is just overwhelming. I'll be sitting on the edge of the bed, just dreading having to get dressed and look at myself in the mirror. \n\nAnd because of that shame, I have started pulling back from things I actually used to love. Like, I used to go to this Saturday morning Vinyasa yoga class with a couple of friends. It was my one sacred hour to just breathe and stretch and get out of my own head. But lately, I've been canceling. I'll text them and say I have a headache or I'm too backed up with work. The truth is, I just feel so gross and bloated and... I don't know, unworthy of being in that space? I just don't want to face anyone. I don't want them to look at me and somehow know what I did the night before. \n\nEven my own private stuff, like my creative time... I used to love painting water colors on Sunday afternoons. Just setting up on the back porch and messing around with colors. It was so relaxing. But now, I just sit on the couch and stare at the TV instead. It's like the eating takes up so much emotional energy, and the guilt takes up whatever is left, that I just don't have the capacity for joy anymore. I just want to hide. So, yeah, I guess I am skipping things. Not because I don't want to do them in theory, but because I just can't bear the thought of being seen when I feel this disgusting.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually try to hide or conceal your eating during these episodes from your family members?\n\nPatient: Hide it? Yeah... yeah, I do. I mean, it's not like I'm sneaking food into my bedroom or anything like that, but I definitely wait until I'm alone. It's... it's like a whole production, really. \n\nLike I was saying about my husband and the arguments... things are already so tense between us. The last thing I want is for him to walk into the kitchen and see me standing there, practically inhaling a family-sized bag of chips or a whole sleeve of cookies. He already thinks I'm checked out, you know? He already complains that I'm not present. If he saw me like that, just totally zoned out, stuffing my face while I'm staring at my phone... I can't even imagine the look on his face. The judgment. It would just be another thing for him to criticize me about, another reason for him to be disappointed. \n\nSo, I wait. I wait until he goes upstairs to take a shower, or if he's out in the garage working on his car, or even if he just runs out to the hardware store for twenty minutes. The minute I hear the door close or the water running, it's like this switch flips. I'll hurry into the pantry, and I'll pull out whatever it is—the chips, the pretzels, the leftover takeout from the night before—and I just go at it. \n\nAnd then, the cleanup... that's a whole other stressful part of it. I have to make sure I hide the evidence. If I finish a box of crackers, I can't just leave the empty box right there in the recycling bin on top where he'll see it. I'll literally shove it down to the bottom, under the newspapers and the junk mail, or sometimes I'll even take the wrappers and bury them in the trash can outside. I know how crazy that sounds, sneaking around my own house to throw away a food wrapper, but I just... I can't let him know. I can't let anyone know. \n\nEven if my daughter comes home early from soccer practice—she's fourteen, you know how observant teenagers are—if I hear the front door open while I'm in the middle of it, I'll literally shove the food back into the cabinet or into the fridge, wipe my mouth, and pretend I was just getting a glass of water. My heart will be pounding, just racing, terrified that she saw me. Because how do you explain that to your kid? \"Sorry, Mom was just eating her feelings because she can't handle her job or her marriage.\" You can't. So yeah, I hide it. I hide it from everyone. It's exhausting, honestly, keeping up this secret double life right in the middle of my own kitchen, but the thought of them seeing me... it's just too humiliating.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight due to these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Weight? You know, it’s funny you ask that because you would think, right? You would absolutely think that with the sheer volume of food I’m consuming during these... these moments in the kitchen, that I would be packing on the pounds. I mean, logically, if you look at the calories in a family-sized bag of chips and a whole sleeve of cookies, or whatever I'm finding in the pantry, it should be adding up. \n\nBut... no, not really. I mean, I haven't had to go out and buy a whole new wardrobe or anything. My work clothes still fit, thank goodness, because I have this one tailored navy blazer that I wear for all the big client pitches, and if that stopped fitting, I think I'd actually have a full-blown panic attack right there in my closet. \n\nI don't weigh myself every day, though. I actually kind of avoid the digital scale we have in the master bathroom because I'm just so terrified of what it *might* say, given what I know I'm doing when nobody is looking. Like, if there's no physical proof on my body, if my husband can't point to a huge weight gain as another thing to criticize, then maybe I can trick myself into thinking it's not as bad as it feels in my head. Because if it happens on a Thursday night after a late client dinner, then Friday is ruined because I'm exhausted and bloated at my desk, and then by Saturday I'm canceling my yoga class because I still feel disgusting. So even if the actual eating episode is only once or twice a week, the ripple effect of it is just... it's always there. It feels like I never really get a break from the cycle of it, you know? It's just always hanging over me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often criticize yourself after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Criticize myself? Oh, God, yes. Constantly. It's almost worse than the eating itself, to be completely honest. Afterward, when the food is gone and I'm just standing there looking at the empty wrappers or the crumbs on the counter... the wave of embarrassment that washes over me is just suffocating. I feel so utterly disgusted with myself. I mean, I will literally look down at my hands and wonder what on earth is wrong with me. \n\nBecause, you have to understand, in every other aspect of my life, I am so put together. It feels like I've just blown that entire persona. Like the disciplined person I try so hard to be is just a total fraud, and it all falls apart the second I am alone in my kitchen.\n\nAnd then the guilt sets in, and I will just sit there at the kitchen island, staring into space, just relentlessly beating myself up. I look at the mess on the counter and think about how I’ve completely blown it. I'm not standing there crying over a breakup or fuming over a rude email from a client. I'm just... there. And then the eating starts. So, I don't know if I'd say it's an emotional response. I haven't really connected it to feeling sad or angry or anything specific like that. It feels more like this weird, automatic thing that just takes over, rather than me trying to soothe some specific emotion. I honestly haven't really thought about it in those terms before.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are triggered by specific emotions, such as anxiety, sadness, or frustration?\n\nPatient: I mean, like I was just saying, I really haven't connected the dots between specific emotions and the eating. You're asking about anxiety, sadness, frustration... and it's just, it's not like that for me. I don't sit there and think, \"Oh, I'm so frustrated that the Johnson account fell through, I'm going to eat a whole sleeve of crackers.\" It's just not this conscious, one-to-one reaction. \n\nI mean, if we're talking about anxiety, well, my baseline is pretty much always a low hum of anxiety, right? Am I frustrated when my junior copywriter misses a deadline for the third time this month? Absolutely. Am I sad? Sometimes, yeah, like anyone else. I'm not doing it to push down a feeling of sadness or to calm my nerves. It's almost like I'm disconnected from my emotions entirely in that moment. I just kind of force myself to suffer through the physical consequences of it, I guess. It's almost like a punishment I feel like I deserve for losing control like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel extremely hungry before these episodes of eating happen?\n\nPatient: Hungry? Well... no, not really. I mean, it's not about hunger. It's almost never about actual, physical hunger. Sometimes I've even just had dinner with my husband—maybe we ordered takeout or I threw together a salad, you know, a completely normal, filling meal. So my stomach isn't growling or anything like that. \n\nBut it's this... this mental hunger, I guess? Or like an emotional craving that just suddenly takes over. I'll be cleaning up the kitchen, wiping down the counters, putting the plates in the dishwasher, and all of a sudden, it's just this overwhelming urge. It's not that my body needs fuel. It's more like my brain is screaming for a distraction or a way to numb out the stress from the day, the tension in the house, the emails I still have to answer for the morning meeting. \n\nSo, no, I wouldn't say I'm extremely hungry. Or even mildly hungry, most of the time. I could be completely full from dinner and still end up standing at the pantry ten minutes later, eating half a box of crackers before I even realize what I'm doing. It's just so frustrating because if it were just hunger, I could fix that, right? I could just eat a proper meal. But it's not. It's something else entirely, and it just feels so impossible to satisfy.\n\nDoctor: Does your eating behavior change when you're under a lot of stress at work?\n\nPatient: Stress? Oh, absolutely. Stress is... it's the whole trigger, really. I mean, my job is always demanding, but lately, it's just been on another level. This whole pattern of coming home and just losing myself in the pantry... it really started when this massive AR/VR campaign ramped up at the agency. We're talking about a multi-million dollar account, and the client is just incredibly demanding. They want constant updates, revisions, new pitch decks every other day. I'm managing a team of twelve, and the pressure is just astronomical. \n\nAnd then, on top of all that pressure at the office, things at home have been so incredibly tense. My husband and I, we've just been arguing constantly. It's about everything and nothing—the house, the finances, the fact that I'm working so much, the fact that he feels like I'm not present even when I am home. It's just this constant, low-level hostility that spikes into these really awful fights. \n\nSo you take the stress from the AR/VR launch, and you combine it with the arguments at home getting so much worse, and it was just... a perfect storm. That was about three months ago. That's when I really noticed this starting. Three months of just coming home, feeling like I've been put through a wringer all day, walking into a house where I feel like I'm walking on eggshells, and then just... heading straight for the kitchen the minute I have a moment alone. It's like the food is the only thing that gives me a break from the pressure, even if it's just for those ten or twenty minutes before the guilt sets in. But yeah, to answer your question, three months ago is when the stress really peaked and this whole nightmare started.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating very quickly during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Quickly? Oh, gosh, yes. It’s... it’s honestly like a blur. I don’t even take the time to sit down at the table or get a proper plate most of the time. I’ll just be standing there, right at the kitchen island, usually with my phone in one hand because I'm just obsessively scrolling through work emails or checking the latest Slack messages from the creative team. And while I’m doing that, reading about whatever new crisis is happening with the pitch deck, I’m just eating so incredibly fast. It's almost frantic. \n\nI shove it in so quickly that I barely even taste it. You'd think if you were going to eat all that food, you'd at least get the pleasure of enjoying the flavor, right? But I don't. I'm not savoring it at all. It's just this mechanical, rapid motion, almost like I'm on autopilot. And it’s like I completely zone out, because sometimes I’ll finally look down, pulling my eyes away from the screen for a second, and the containers are just... empty. The whole box or the entire bag, just completely gone. And I just stand there staring at the empty packaging because I haven’t even really registered what I was eating. It happens so fast that my brain doesn't even have time to catch up with what my hands and my mouth were doing. I'll just be standing there in the kitchen, holding an empty container, wondering how on earth I just ate all of that in a matter of minutes.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you used to enjoy because of your eating habits?\n\nPatient: Lost interest? I don't know if \"lost interest\" is the right phrase, but... it's definitely affecting what I do. I mean, work is still happening. I'm still showing up for the agency, still leading the team meetings, still pushing through the AR/VR campaign because I have to. There's too much money on the line for me to just drop the ball there. I can put on the blazer and the professional face and get through the day. \n\nBut the mornings after these nights... after I've just stood in the kitchen and eaten everything... I wake up feeling so incredibly drained. It's this heavy, sluggish feeling, like a hangover, but worse because it's entirely my own fault. And the shame is just overwhelming. I'll be sitting on the edge of the bed, just dreading having to get dressed and look at myself in the mirror. \n\nAnd because of that shame, I have started pulling back from things I actually used to love. Like, I used to go to this Saturday morning Vinyasa yoga class with a couple of friends. It was my one sacred hour to just breathe and stretch and get out of my own head. But lately, I've been canceling. I'll text them and say I have a headache or I'm too backed up with work. The truth is, I just feel so gross and bloated and... I don't know, unworthy of being in that space? I just don't want to face anyone. I don't want them to look at me and somehow know what I did the night before. \n\nEven my own private stuff, like my creative time... I used to love painting water colors on Sunday afternoons. Just setting up on the back porch and messing around with colors. It was so relaxing. But now, I just sit on the couch and stare at the TV instead. It's like the eating takes up so much emotional energy, and the guilt takes up whatever is left, that I just don't have the capacity for joy anymore. I just want to hide. So, yeah, I guess I am skipping things. Not because I don't want to do them in theory, but because I just can't bear the thought of being seen when I feel this disgusting.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually try to hide or conceal your eating during these episodes from your family members?\n\nPatient: Hide it? Yeah... yeah, I do. I mean, it's not like I'm sneaking food into my bedroom or anything like that, but I definitely wait until I'm alone. It's... it's like a whole production, really. \n\nLike I was saying about my husband and the arguments... things are already so tense between us. The last thing I want is for him to walk into the kitchen and see me standing there, practically inhaling a family-sized bag of chips or a whole sleeve of cookies. He already thinks I'm checked out, you know? He already complains that I'm not present. If he saw me like that, just totally zoned out, stuffing my face while I'm staring at my phone... I can't even imagine the look on his face. The judgment. It would just be another thing for him to criticize me about, another reason for him to be disappointed. \n\nSo, I wait. I wait until he goes upstairs to take a shower, or if he's out in the garage working on his car, or even if he just runs out to the hardware store for twenty minutes. The minute I hear the door close or the water running, it's like this switch flips. I'll hurry into the pantry, and I'll pull out whatever it is—the chips, the pretzels, the leftover takeout from the night before—and I just go at it. \n\nAnd then, the cleanup... that's a whole other stressful part of it. I have to make sure I hide the evidence. If I finish a box of crackers, I can't just leave the empty box right there in the recycling bin on top where he'll see it. I'll literally shove it down to the bottom, under the newspapers and the junk mail, or sometimes I'll even take the wrappers and bury them in the trash can outside. I know how crazy that sounds, sneaking around my own house to throw away a food wrapper, but I just... I can't let him know. I can't let anyone know. \n\nEven if my daughter comes home early from soccer practice—she's fourteen, you know how observant teenagers are—if I hear the front door open while I'm in the middle of it, I'll literally shove the food back into the cabinet or into the fridge, wipe my mouth, and pretend I was just getting a glass of water. My heart will be pounding, just racing, terrified that she saw me. Because how do you explain that to your kid? \"Sorry, Mom was just eating her feelings because she can't handle her job or her marriage.\" You can't. So yeah, I hide it. I hide it from everyone. It's exhausting, honestly, keeping up this secret double life right in the middle of my own kitchen, but the thought of them seeing me... it's just too humiliating.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight due to these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Weight? You know, it’s funny you ask that because you would think, right? You would absolutely think that with the sheer volume of food I’m consuming during these... these moments in the kitchen, that I would be packing on the pounds. I mean, logically, if you look at the calories in a family-sized bag of chips and a whole sleeve of cookies, or whatever I'm finding in the pantry, it should be adding up. \n\nBut... no, not really. I mean, I haven't had to go out and buy a whole new wardrobe or anything. My work clothes still fit, thank goodness, because I have this one tailored navy blazer that I wear for all the big client pitches, and if that stopped fitting, I think I'd actually have a full-blown panic attack right there in my closet. \n\nI don't weigh myself every day, though. I actually kind of avoid the digital scale we have in the master bathroom because I'm just so terrified of what it *might* say, given what I know I'm doing when nobody is looking. I'll just walk right past it. But when I went to my primary care doctor for a regular checkup a few months ago, my weight was pretty much exactly the same as it was the year before. Maybe it fluctuates a pound or two depending on the time of the month or water weight, but nothing significant at all. \n\nIt's almost a cruel irony, really. Because on the one hand, I'm relieved—obviously, I'm incredibly relieved that I'm not gaining massive amounts of weight. But on the other hand, it almost makes it easier for me to stay in denial? Like, if there's no physical proof on my body, if my husband can't point to a huge weight gain as another thing to criticize, then maybe I can trick myself into thinking it's not as bad as it feels in my head. But then I wake up feeling so bloated and sluggish, like I mentioned before, and I just know it's taking a toll on my body internally, even if the scale isn't moving. So, I guess the short answer is no, no major changes in the actual number, which is honestly a miracle considering the sheer amount of food I'm putting away.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of eating happen?\n\nPatient: How often? Gosh, um... if I really sit back and think about it, looking at the calendar... it's been a solid three months of this. Like, really consistently for the last three months. I mean, I think it really ramped up around the time we got the new quarterly projections at the agency and my husband started getting more vocal about my hours. \n\nSo, yeah, for the last three months, I'd say there is at least one night, almost every single week, where I end up in the kitchen doing exactly what I described. Just eating like that until I can't anymore. And honestly, some weeks it's worse. Some weeks it happens twice. \n\nIt almost always follows a specific pattern, too. It's never just a random, peaceful Tuesday where everything was fine. It's pretty much always right after a tense argument with my husband—you know, one of those fights where he gives me that heavy sigh and just walks away upstairs—or it's after a really late work event. Like, if I've been at the agency smiling and pitching that AR/VR campaign until 8 or 9 PM, and I just come home completely, utterly depleted. I'll walk in the door, the house is quiet, he's already asleep or ignoring me, and I just... I gravitate right to the pantry. \n\nAnd I know once a week, or twice a week, might not sound like an everyday, constant thing on paper, but when you factor in the emotional toll? The hangover feeling the next morning, the sneaking around, the shame I was telling you about... it feels like it consumes my entire week. Because if it happens on a Thursday night after a late client dinner, then Friday is ruined because I'm exhausted and bloated at my desk, and then by Saturday I'm canceling my yoga class because I still feel disgusting. So even if the actual eating episode is only once or twice a week, the ripple effect of it is just... it's always there. It feels like I never really get a break from the cycle of it, you know? It's just always hanging over me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often criticize yourself after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Criticize myself? Oh, God, yes. Constantly. It's almost worse than the eating itself, to be completely honest. Afterward, when the food is gone and I'm just standing there looking at the empty wrappers or the crumbs on the counter... the wave of embarrassment that washes over me is just suffocating. I feel so utterly disgusted with myself. I mean, I will literally look down at my hands and wonder what on earth is wrong with me. \n\nBecause, you have to understand, in every other aspect of my life, I am so put together. I manage a whole team at the agency, I handle massive budgets, I'm the one people come to when they need a crisis solved right that second. I really pride myself on being this highly disciplined, capable woman. And then I have these moments where I completely lose control over a bag of pretzels or a box of cookies? It feels like I've just blown that entire persona. Like the disciplined person I try so hard to be is just a total fraud, and it all falls apart the second I am alone in my kitchen.\n\nAnd then the guilt sets in, and I will just sit there at the kitchen island, staring into space, just relentlessly beating myself up. I will run through all the things I should have done instead of eating. I will sit there thinking I should have just gone for a run to blow off steam, or I should have unrolled my mat and done some yoga in the living room to decompress. Why couldn't I just do some deep breathing like a normal stressed person, you know? But instead, I did this. And that self-criticism just loops in my head for hours. It bleeds into the next day. I will be sitting in a morning meeting, reviewing ad copy, and in the back of my mind, I am just tearing myself apart, calling myself weak and disgusting over and over again. It is just a relentless inner monologue.\n\nDoctor: Do you criticize yourself immediately after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Immediately? Yes, exactly, like I was just saying, it’s... it is instant. There is absolutely zero delay. The very second I swallow the last bite, or the moment my hand hits the bottom of the empty bag... boom. It hits me like a freight train. \n\nThat embarrassment and disgust I was just telling you about? It doesn't wait until the next morning or even until I go upstairs to bed. It is right there, immediately. I'll literally still be standing there in the kitchen, the taste still in my mouth, and I just feel so profoundly embarrassed. I look at the mess on the counter and think about how I’ve completely blown it. Like I said, I try so incredibly hard all day long to be this highly disciplined, put-together person who has all the answers for everyone else, and in that immediate aftermath, I just feel like a total failure. The contrast between who I am at 2 PM and who I am at 10 PM is just jarring.\n\nAnd right then and there, without even leaving the kitchen, I'll start the spiral. I'll just sit there at the island, staring at the empty wrappers, immediately punishing myself. I'll sit there thinking, \"Why didn't I just go for a run? I could have just put on my sneakers and jogged around the neighborhood to clear my head.\" Or I'll look over at the living room and think, \"I have that expensive yoga mat rolled up right there in the corner, why didn't I just go do some yoga instead of doing this?\" It's instantaneous. There is no buffer at all between the end of the eating and the absolute disgust with myself. It's just an immediate crash.\n\nDoctor: Does the self-criticism you feel after these episodes of eating tend to last for a while after you've gone to bed, or even into the next day?\n\nPatient: Oh, it definitely lasts. It doesn't just stop when I finally drag myself upstairs to bed, not at all. I'll lie there in the dark, staring at the ceiling, my husband asleep next to me, and my brain is just... it's just spinning. Replaying the whole thing. And then, yes, it absolutely bleeds into the next day. It's like this heavy, dark cloud that I wake up under. \n\nI mean, I'm still keeping up with work, thank goodness. I still show up at the agency, I still run the meetings, I still smile and nod and approve the ad copy. I haven't let the balls drop there, because I can't. I'm the manager, I have to be on. But underneath that? The mornings after these nights, I just feel completely drained. It's this bone-deep exhaustion, mixed with just... so much shame. I'll be sitting at my desk, looking at spreadsheets, and I just feel gross in my own skin. \n\nAnd it's starting to affect the things I actually enjoy doing, too. Like, I was telling you about my yoga class? I used to love going to this Saturday morning vinyasa flow. It was my time to just breathe and reset. But lately, if I've had one of these episodes on a Thursday or Friday, I'll just cancel. I'll wake up Saturday morning, still feeling that hangover of shame and bloat, and I just... I can't do it. I don't want to put on the tight leggings, I don't want to be in a room full of people, I just don't want to face anyone. I've even started skipping my weekend creative time, where I usually sketch or brainstorm personal projects, because my mind is just so bogged down by the guilt. I just want to hide. So, yeah, it definitely doesn't just stay in the kitchen. It follows me around for days.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating in response to your emotions during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Emotions? I... I don't know. I mean, I guess it's possible, but I really haven't noticed a clear pattern like that. It's not like I have a bad meeting and immediately think, \"I need to go eat a box of cookies.\" \n\nI mean, sure, I'm stressed. Who isn't stressed in my line of work? I manage a huge team, the clients are always demanding revisions at the eleventh hour, the budgets are tight... it's a pressure cooker. And I'm the one everyone looks to for answers. So, yes, there's stress. But I don't feel like I'm eating *because* I'm stressed, if that makes sense? \n\nIt's more like... it just happens. I'll get home, I'll put my bag down, I'll start sorting through the mail or wiping down the counter, and then suddenly I'm just... eating. I'm not standing there crying over a breakup or fuming over a rude email from a client. I'm just... there. And then the eating starts. So, I don't know if I'd say it's an emotional response. I haven't really connected it to feeling sad or angry or anything specific like that. It feels more like this weird, automatic thing that just takes over, rather than me trying to soothe some specific emotion. I honestly haven't really thought about it in those terms before.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are triggered by specific emotions, such as anxiety, sadness, or frustration?\n\nPatient: I mean, like I was just saying, I really haven't connected the dots between specific emotions and the eating. You're asking about anxiety, sadness, frustration... and it's just, it's not like that for me. I don't sit there and think, \"Oh, I'm so frustrated that the Johnson account fell through, I'm going to eat a whole sleeve of crackers.\" It's just not this conscious, one-to-one reaction. \n\nI mean, if we're talking about anxiety, well, my baseline is pretty much always a low hum of anxiety, right? I'm a marketing manager, I'm 40 years old, I'm trying to keep a dozen plates spinning at once, both at the agency and at home. So, am I anxious? Sure. Am I frustrated when my junior copywriter misses a deadline for the third time this month? Absolutely. Am I sad? Sometimes, yeah, like anyone else. But I don't feel like those feelings are the *trigger*. \n\nIt's more like... I'll just be going through the motions of my evening. Like, maybe it's a Tuesday, and I've just finished a two-hour Zoom call that could have been an email, and I'm tired, but I'm not actively, intensely feeling a specific emotion. I'm just existing. I'll walk into the kitchen to maybe get a glass of water, and then it's like a switch flips. Suddenly I'm opening the pantry, and then the fridge, and it's just... happening. I'm not doing it to push down a feeling of sadness or to calm my nerves. It's almost like I'm disconnected from my emotions entirely in that moment. It's just this incredibly powerful, automatic drive that takes over, completely separate from whatever I might have been feeling five minutes prior. So, no, I really don't think it's triggered by specific emotions like that. It just doesn't feel like an emotional response to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Well, let me think about how that actually adds up over a month. If I really look back at my schedule, especially over the last three months or so, it’s been pretty consistent. I'd say there’s been at least one night almost every single week where I end up eating like this. And honestly, some weeks it’s worse, and it happens twice. So if you’re looking for a monthly total, I guess we’re talking anywhere from four to maybe eight times a month? 

It’s funny, as I’m saying this out loud and trying to pinpoint exactly *when* it happens... I know I just went on and on about how I don't think it's triggered by my feelings, but I am noticing a bit of a situational pattern now that I'm really thinking about the timeline. Like, it almost always seems to happen after an argument with my husband—usually over something completely ridiculous, like him leaving his golf clubs in the hallway or me being snappy because the kitchen wasn't cleaned up—or it happens after a really late work event. 

You know the kind of events I mean, those industry mixers or client dinners that are supposed to end at eight but always drag on until nearly ten. I have to sit there making small talk, and then I finally walk through my front door, take off my heels, and the house is totally quiet. It's almost like the disruption to my evening routine is what derails me. I get home late, or we have a stupid bickering match, and my normal schedule is just blown to pieces, and then... there I am in the pantry again. It's not that I'm standing there crying about the argument or stressing over the client, it's just that my evening got thrown off track. So, yeah, to answer your question, at least once a week, sometimes twice, pretty steadily for the last three months now.

I will sit there thinking I should have just gone for a run to blow off steam, or I should have unrolled my mat and done some yoga in the living room to decompress. Why couldn't I just do some deep breathing like a normal stressed person, you know? But instead, I did this. And that self-criticism just loops in my head for hours. It bleeds into the next day. I will be sitting in a morning meeting, reviewing ad copy, and in the back of my mind, I am just tearing myself apart, calling myself weak and disgusting over and over again. It is just a relentless inner monologue.\n\nDoctor: Do you criticize yourself immediately after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Immediately? Yes, exactly, like I was just saying, it’s... it is instant. There is absolutely zero delay. The very second I swallow the last bite, or the moment my hand hits the bottom of the empty bag... boom. It hits me like a freight train. \n\nThat embarrassment and disgust I was just telling you about? It doesn't wait until the next morning or even until I go upstairs to bed. It is right there, immediately. I'll literally still be standing there in the kitchen, the taste still in my mouth, and I just feel so profoundly embarrassed. I look at the mess on the counter and think about how I’ve completely blown it. Like I said, I try so incredibly hard all day long to be this highly disciplined, put-together person who has all the answers for everyone else, and in that immediate aftermath, I just feel like a total failure. The contrast between who I am at 2 PM and who I am at 10 PM is just jarring.\n\nAnd right then and there, without even leaving the kitchen, I'll start the spiral. I'll just sit there at the island, staring at the empty wrappers, immediately punishing myself. I'll sit there thinking, \"Why didn't I just go for a run? There is no buffer at all between the end of the eating and the absolute disgust with myself. It's just an immediate crash.\n\nDoctor: Does the self-criticism you feel after these episodes of eating tend to last for a while after you've gone to bed, or even into the next day?\n\nPatient: Oh, it definitely lasts. It doesn't just stop when I finally drag myself upstairs to bed, not at all. I'll be sitting at my desk, looking at spreadsheets, and I just feel gross in my own skin. \n\nAnd it's starting to affect the things I actually enjoy doing, too. Like, I was telling you about my yoga class? I used to love going to this Saturday morning vinyasa flow. It was my time to just breathe and reset. I don't want to put on the tight leggings, I don't want to be in a room full of people, I just don't want to face anyone. It follows me around for days.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating in response to your emotions during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Emotions? I... I don't know. It's not like I have a bad meeting and immediately think, \"I need to go eat a box of cookies.\" \n\nI mean, sure, I'm stressed. Who isn't stressed in my line of work? I manage a huge team, the clients are always demanding revisions at the eleventh hour, the budgets are tight... it's a pressure cooker. And I'm the one everyone looks to for answers. I haven't really connected it to feeling sad or angry or anything specific like that. It feels more like this weird, automatic thing that just takes over, rather than me trying to soothe some specific emotion. You're asking about anxiety, sadness, frustration... and it's just, it's not like that for me. I'll walk into the kitchen to maybe get a glass of water, and then it's like a switch flips. Suddenly I'm opening the pantry, and then the fridge, and it's just... happening. I'm not doing it to push down a feeling of sadness or to calm my nerves. It's almost like I'm disconnected from my emotions entirely in that moment. It's just this incredibly powerful, automatic drive that takes over, completely separate from whatever I might have been feeling five minutes prior. So, no, I really don't think it's triggered by specific emotions like that. It just doesn't feel like an emotional response to me.\n\nDoctor: How many times a month would you say these episodes of eating happen?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think about how that actually adds up over a month. If I really look back at my schedule, especially over the last three months or so, it’s been pretty consistent. I'd say there’s been at least one night almost every single week where I end up eating like this. And honestly, some weeks it’s worse, and it happens twice. It's almost like the disruption to my evening routine is what derails me. I get home late, or we have a stupid bickering match, and my normal schedule is just blown to pieces, and then... there I am in the pantry again. It's not that I'm standing there crying about the argument or stressing over the client, it's just that my evening got thrown off track. The contrast between who I am at 2 PM and who I am at 10 PM is just jarring.\n\nAnd right then and there, without even leaving the kitchen, I'll start the spiral. I'll just sit there at the island, staring at the empty wrappers, immediately punishing myself. I'll sit there thinking, \"Why didn't I just go for a run? I could have just put on my sneakers and jogged around the neighborhood to clear my head.\" Or I'll look over at the living room and think, \"I have that expensive yoga mat rolled up right there in the corner, why didn't I just go do some yoga instead of doing this?\" It's instantaneous. There is no buffer at all between the end of the eating and the absolute disgust with myself. It's just an immediate crash.\n\nDoctor: Does the self-criticism you feel after these episodes of eating tend to last for a while after you've gone to bed, or even into the next day?\n\nPatient: Oh, it definitely lasts. It doesn't just stop when I finally drag myself upstairs to bed, not at all. I'll lie there in the dark, staring at the ceiling, my husband asleep next to me, and my brain is just... it's just spinning. Replaying the whole thing. And then, yes, it absolutely bleeds into the next day. It's like this heavy, dark cloud that I wake up under. \n\nI mean, I'm still keeping up with work, thank goodness. I still show up at the agency, I still run the meetings, I still smile and nod and approve the ad copy. I haven't let the balls drop there, because I can't. I'm the manager, I have to be on. But underneath that? The mornings after these nights, I just feel completely drained. It's this bone-deep exhaustion, mixed with just... so much shame. I'll be sitting at my desk, looking at spreadsheets, and I just feel gross in my own skin. \n\nAnd it's starting to affect the things I actually enjoy doing, too. Like, I was telling you about my yoga class? I used to love going to this Saturday morning vinyasa flow. It was my time to just breathe and reset. But lately, if I've had one of these episodes on a Thursday or Friday, I'll just cancel. I'll wake up Saturday morning, still feeling that hangover of shame and bloat, and I just... I can't do it. I don't want to put on the tight leggings, I don't want to be in a room full of people, I just don't want to face anyone. I've even started skipping my weekend creative time, where I usually sketch or brainstorm personal projects, because my mind is just so bogged down by the guilt. I just want to hide. So, yeah, it definitely doesn't just stay in the kitchen. It follows me around for days.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating in response to your emotions during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Emotions? I... I don't know. I mean, I guess it's possible, but I really haven't noticed a clear pattern like that. It's not like I have a bad meeting and immediately think, \"I need to go eat a box of cookies.\" \n\nI mean, sure, I'm stressed. Who isn't stressed in my line of work? I manage a huge team, the clients are always demanding revisions at the eleventh hour, the budgets are tight... it's a pressure cooker. And I'm the one everyone looks to for answers. So, yes, there's stress. But I don't feel like I'm eating *because* I'm stressed, if that makes sense? \n\nIt's more like... it just happens. I'll get home, I'll put my bag down, I'll start sorting through the mail or wiping down the counter, and then suddenly I'm just... eating. I'm not standing there crying over a breakup or fuming over a rude email from a client. I'm just... there. And then the eating starts. So, I don't know if I'd say it's an emotional response. I haven't really connected it to feeling sad or angry or anything specific like that. It feels more like this weird, automatic thing that just takes over, rather than me trying to soothe some specific emotion. I honestly haven't really thought about it in those terms before.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating episodes are triggered by specific emotions, such as anxiety, sadness, or frustration?\n\nPatient: I mean, like I was just saying, I really haven't connected the dots between specific emotions and the eating. You're asking about anxiety, sadness, frustration... and it's just, it's not like that for me. I don't sit there and think, \"Oh, I'm so frustrated that the Johnson account fell through, I'm going to eat a whole sleeve of crackers.\" It's just not this conscious, one-to-one reaction. \n\nI mean, if we're talking about anxiety, well, my baseline is pretty much always a low hum of anxiety, right? I'm a marketing manager, I'm 40 years old, I'm trying to keep a dozen plates spinning at once, both at the agency and at home. So, am I anxious? Sure. Am I frustrated when my junior copywriter misses a deadline for the third time this month? Absolutely. Am I sad? Sometimes, yeah, like anyone else. But I don't feel like those feelings are the *trigger*. \n\nIt's more like... I'll just be going through the motions of my evening. Like, maybe it's a Tuesday, and I've just finished a two-hour Zoom call that could have been an email, and I'm tired, but I'm not actively, intensely feeling a specific emotion. I'm just existing. I'll walk into the kitchen to maybe get a glass of water, and then it's like a switch flips. Suddenly I'm opening the pantry, and then the fridge, and it's just... happening. I'm not doing it to push down a feeling of sadness or to calm my nerves. It's almost like I'm disconnected from my emotions entirely in that moment. It's just this incredibly powerful, automatic drive that takes over, completely separate from whatever I might have been feeling five minutes prior. So, no, I really don't think it's triggered by specific emotions like that. It just doesn't feel like an emotional response to me.\n\nDoctor: How many times a month would you say these episodes of eating happen?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think about how that actually adds up over a month. If I really look back at my schedule, especially over the last three months or so, it’s been pretty consistent. I'd say there’s been at least one night almost every single week where I end up eating like this. And honestly, some weeks it’s worse, and it happens twice. So if you’re looking for a monthly total, I guess we’re talking anywhere from four to maybe eight times a month? \n\nIt’s funny, as I’m saying this out loud and trying to pinpoint exactly *when* it happens... I know I just went on and on about how I don't think it's triggered by my feelings, but I am noticing a bit of a situational pattern now that I'm really thinking about the timeline. Like, it almost always seems to happen after an argument with my husband—usually over something completely ridiculous, like him leaving his golf clubs in the hallway or me being snappy because the kitchen wasn't cleaned up—or it happens after a really late work event. \n\nYou know the kind of events I mean, those industry mixers or client dinners that are supposed to end at eight but always drag on until nearly ten. I have to sit there making small talk, and then I finally walk through my front door, take off my heels, and the house is totally quiet. It's almost like the disruption to my evening routine is what derails me. I get home late, or we have a stupid bickering match, and my normal schedule is just blown to pieces, and then... there I am in the pantry again. It's not that I'm standing there crying about the argument or stressing over the client, it's just that my evening got thrown off track. So, yeah, to answer your question, at least once a week, sometimes twice, pretty steadily for the last three months now.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Unable to stop... yeah. Yes, actually, that's exactly what it is. It’s the strangest, most frustrating feeling, honestly, because in every other aspect of my life, I am so in control. I mean, I have to be, right? I direct a whole department, I manage budgets, I make a hundred executive decisions before noon. But when this happens in the kitchen... it's like a complete disconnect. 

Once I start—and especially, like I was just saying, after a really tense night with my husband where we've been sniping at each other over something silly—it feels like the entire decision-making part of my brain just completely goes offline. I'll be standing there, and maybe I just meant to have a couple of crackers to settle my stomach, but then my hands are just moving on their own. I'm opening a box of cookies, then I'm grabbing a handful of almonds, then I'm digging into a bag of chips. 

And the crazy part is, I'm completely aware of it happening. The whole time, there's this rational voice in the back of my head. I'm literally standing there thinking to myself, "Patricia, stop, this is enough. You don't even want this. Put the box down." But I just keep reaching for more. It's like I'm a passenger in my own body just watching it happen. I can hear myself pleading to just close the pantry door and walk away, but the physical action just overrides my logic entirely. The brakes just completely fail, and I can't seem to make myself stop once that momentum gets going.

I always tell myself, \"I’m just having some leftovers, just a little taste to wind down.\" But then... I don't even know how to explain it, I just sort of lose track of everything. The other night, for example, I realized I’d eaten half a massive tray of catered pasta I brought home from a luncheon, and then I moved right on to the leftover dessert from a client dinner, and then I just stood there eating whatever snacks were in the pantry—my kids' cookies, pretzels, just whatever I could get my hands on. \n\nAnd I eat all of it in one sitting. It's an enormous amount of food, and it's happening so often now, and I just feel this terrible guilt afterward. I just... No. No, not even a little bit. It is actually terrifying how little control I have in those moments. We have been bickering a lot lately, mostly because I am so stressed from the agency and he feels like I am not present, which just creates this awful, heavy atmosphere in the house. I just keep opening wrappers or scooping out more food, completely ignoring my own internal voice begging me to quit. I just can't seem to halt the momentum once it gets going, no matter how hard I try to reason with myself in the moment. I just watch myself doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these episodes of eating?\n\nPatient: Guilty? Oh, yes. The guilt is... it's absolutely overwhelming. And it's not just guilt, it's this profound sense of embarrassment and just utter disgust with myself. It hits me the second I stop eating, like a ton of bricks. I'll be standing there in the kitchen, surrounded by all the empty wrappers and Tupperware containers, and I just look at the mess and think, \"What have I done?\" \n\nI mean, I spend my entire day at the agency projecting this image of someone who is totally put together, disciplined, in charge. I'm the one managing the budgets, directing the campaigns, keeping everyone else on track. And then I come home and do this? It feels like I've completely blown that entire persona, like the disciplined person I try so hard to be all day is just a total fraud. \n\nI'll just sit there at the kitchen counter, feeling physically sick and emotionally drained, and my mind just starts racing with all these \"should haves.\" I'll sit there thinking, \"Patricia, you should have just gone for a run when you got home. You should have gone upstairs and done 20 minutes of yoga. You could have read a book, taken a bath, done literally anything else.\" But instead, I just stood there and ate thousands of calories in secret. And the worst part is knowing that I'm going to have to wake up the next morning, put on my suit, and pretend like none of it happened. It's just this cycle of disgust that I can't seem to break out of, and it hangs over me the entire next day.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after one of these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: No, no, I've never done that. I mean, I know that's something people do, and honestly, sometimes when I'm sitting there feeling so incredibly full and just physically sick from all the food, the thought crosses my mind that maybe it would make the physical discomfort go away. Because the fullness is just so intense, you know? It physically hurts. My stomach gets so bloated and tight, and I just feel so heavy and sluggish, like I can barely move from the kitchen stool. \n\nBut no, I have never actually made myself throw up. I just... I couldn't bring myself to do it. Even though I feel so disgusted with myself, and I desperately want to undo what I just did, I just end up sitting there with it. I sit there with the physical pain and the emotional guilt. I'll usually just end up drinking a huge glass of water, which probably makes the bloating worse, honestly, and then I just drag myself upstairs to bed and lie there feeling awful until I finally fall asleep. I just kind of force myself to suffer through the physical consequences of it, I guess. It's almost like a punishment I feel like I deserve for losing control like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel extremely hungry before these episodes of eating happen?\n\nPatient: Hungry? Well... no, not really. I mean, it's not about hunger. It's almost never about actual, physical hunger. Sometimes I've even just had dinner with my husband—maybe we ordered takeout or I threw together a salad, you know, a completely normal, filling meal. So my stomach isn't growling or anything like that. \n\nBut it's this... this mental hunger, I guess? Or like an emotional craving that just suddenly takes over. I'll be cleaning up the kitchen, wiping down the counters, putting the plates in the dishwasher, and all of a sudden, it's just this overwhelming urge. It's not that my body needs fuel. It's more like my brain is screaming for a distraction or a way to numb out the stress from the day, the tension in the house, the emails I still have to answer for the morning meeting. \n\nSo, no, I wouldn't say I'm extremely hungry. Or even mildly hungry, most of the time. I could be completely full from dinner and still end up standing at the pantry ten minutes later, eating half a box of crackers before I even realize what I'm doing. It's just so frustrating because if it were just hunger, I could fix that, right? I could just eat a proper meal. But it's not. It's something else entirely, and it just feels so impossible to satisfy.\n\nDoctor: Does your eating behavior change when you're under a lot of stress at work?\n\nPatient: Stress? Oh, absolutely. Stress is... it's the whole trigger, really. I mean, my job is always demanding, but lately, it's just been on another level. This whole pattern of coming home and just losing myself in the pantry... it really started when this massive AR/VR campaign ramped up at the agency. We're talking about a multi-million dollar account, and the client is just incredibly demanding. They want constant updates, revisions, new pitch decks every other day. I'm managing a team of twelve, and the pressure is just astronomical. \n\nAnd then, on top of all that pressure at the office, things at home have been so incredibly tense. My husband and I, we've just been arguing constantly. It's about everything and nothing—the house, the finances, the fact that I'm working so much, the fact that he feels like I'm not present even when I am home. It's just this constant, low-level hostility that spikes into these really awful fights. \n\nSo you take the stress from the AR/VR launch, and you combine it with the arguments at home getting so much worse, and it was just... a perfect storm. That was about three months ago. That's when I really noticed this starting. Three months of just coming home, feeling like I've been put through a wringer all day, walking into a house where I feel like I'm walking on eggshells, and then just... heading straight for the kitchen the minute I have a moment alone. It's like the food is the only thing that gives me a break from the pressure, even if it's just for those ten or twenty minutes before the guilt sets in. But yeah, to answer your question, three months ago is when the stress really peaked and this whole nightmare started.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating very quickly during these episodes?\n\nPatient: Quickly? Oh, gosh, yes. It’s... it’s honestly like a blur. I don’t even take the time to sit down at the table or get a proper plate most of the time. I’ll just be standing there, right at the kitchen island, usually with my phone in one hand because I'm just obsessively scrolling through work emails or checking the latest Slack messages from the creative team. And while I’m doing that, reading about whatever new crisis is happening with the pitch deck, I’m just eating so incredibly fast. It's almost frantic. \n\nI shove it in so quickly that I barely even taste it. You'd think if you were going to eat all that food, you'd at least get the pleasure of enjoying the flavor, right? But I don't. I'm not savoring it at all. It's just this mechanical, rapid motion, almost like I'm on autopilot. And it’s like I completely zone out, because sometimes I’ll finally look down, pulling my eyes away from the screen for a second, and the containers are just... empty. The whole box or the entire bag, just completely gone. And I just stand there staring at the empty packaging because I haven’t even really registered what I was eating. It happens so fast that my brain doesn't even have time to catch up with what my hands and my mouth were doing. I'll just be standing there in the kitchen, holding an empty container, wondering how on earth I just ate all of that in a matter of minutes.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you used to enjoy because of your eating habits?\n\nPatient: Lost interest? I don't know if \"lost interest\" is the right phrase, but... it's definitely affecting what I do. I mean, work is still happening. I'm still showing up for the agency, still leading the team meetings, still pushing through the AR/VR campaign because I have to. There's too much money on the line for me to just drop the ball there. I can put on the blazer and the professional face and get through the day. \n\nBut the mornings after these nights... after I've just stood in the kitchen and eaten everything... I'll be sitting on the edge of the bed, just dreading having to get dressed and look at myself in the mirror. \n\nAnd because of that shame, I have started pulling back from things I actually used to love. Like, I used to go to this Saturday morning Vinyasa yoga class with a couple of friends. It was my one sacred hour to just breathe and stretch and get out of my own head. I don't want them to look at me and somehow know what I did the night before. \n\nEven my own private stuff, like my creative time... I used to love painting water colors on Sunday afternoons. Just setting up on the back porch and messing around with colors. It was so relaxing. But now, I just sit on the couch and stare at the TV instead. It's like the eating takes up so much emotional energy, and the guilt takes up whatever is left, that I just don't have the capacity for joy anymore. I just want to hide. So, yeah, I guess I am skipping things. Not because I don't want to do them in theory, but because I just can't bear the thought of being seen when I feel this disgusting.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually try to hide or conceal your eating during these episodes from your family members?\n\nPatient: Hide it? Yeah... yeah, I do. I mean, it's not like I'm sneaking food into my bedroom or anything like that, but I definitely wait until I'm alone. It's... it's like a whole production, really. \n\nLike I was saying about my husband and the arguments... things are already so tense between us. The last thing I want is for him to walk into the kitchen and see me standing there, practically inhaling a family-sized bag of chips or a whole sleeve of cookies. He already thinks I'm checked out, you know? He already complains that I'm not present. If he saw me like that, just totally zoned out, stuffing my face while I'm staring at my phone... I wait until he goes upstairs to take a shower, or if he's out in the garage working on his car, or even if he just runs out to the hardware store for twenty minutes. I have to make sure I hide the evidence. If I finish a box of crackers, I can't just leave the empty box right there in the recycling bin on top where he'll see it. I can't let anyone know. \n\nEven if my daughter comes home early from soccer practice—she's fourteen, you know how observant teenagers are—if I hear the front door open while I'm in the middle of it, I'll literally shove the food back into the cabinet or into the fridge, wipe my mouth, and pretend I was just getting a glass of water. It's exhausting, honestly, keeping up this secret double life right in the middle of my own kitchen, but the thought of them seeing me... it's just too humiliating.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight due to these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Weight?
